# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-23

| **[Agentic World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30136v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent world action models provide visual foresight and future prediction for robot manipulation, but lack explicit mechanisms for compositional skill reuse. Agentic robotics combines code-based planning and reasoning with reusable skills, but often lacks predictive grounding in physical dynamics. We introduce AW-0, an agentic world action model framework that integrates code-based planning with joint video and action prediction. We jointly model code, future video, and robot actions as three coupled generative streams within a unified mixture-of-transformers architecture. Within this architecture a code expert generates parameterized skill routines and control logic while video and action experts generate future visual states and low-level motor commands. Code serves two complementary roles: it structures multi-step prediction and control within the world model and represents skills as reusable programs for subsequent agentic execution. We further introduce an automated curation pipeline that labels robot and human video trajectories with aligned programs and consolidates recurring routines into reusable skill libraries. Evaluations on RoboTwin 2.0 and physical bimanual manipulation tasks show improved task performance over VLA and WAM baselines, alongside gains in language following, semantic reasoning and robustness, demonstrating that agentic code enables connection between planning, prediction and execution within WAMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://agentic-wam.github.io/</p></details> |
| **[World of SWECraft: Agent Collaboration at Scale via Simulated Software Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24061v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As teams of AI agents take on larger projects and long-standing open problems, agent collaboration offers another dimension for scaling inference-time compute. To understand what makes such collaboration effective and scalable, we develop Software World, a framework for simulating multi-agent coworking in realistic software ecosystems. It comes with a pipeline to create environments of real, interdependent Python packages of different topics, sizes, and dependency structures, which we call software worlds. In a simulation, agents maintain packages in the world with a high-level goal of improving software efficiency, and they are free to discover tasks to work on and collaborate with others. We evaluate agents' work in a world collectively by measuring speedup in hidden dependent packages. The framework allows us to study different elements of collaboration by varying communication, ownership, and resource allocation. We run simulations in three different worlds, totaling more than 10,000 hours of agent work across eight models. Overall, performance improvement scales with inference-time compute, with the best model improving downstream efficiency by up to 31%. We show that most agents can find meaningful tasks via collaboration, but some frontier models can prematurely stop looking for improvements and make little progress. We release the simulation framework and traces to support future studies of agent collaboration at https://szj.io/sw.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 9 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Quant-Arena: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Quantitative Trading Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30113v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantitative research is an iterative loop: a researcher proposes a market hypothesis, implements it as an executable signal, tests it on historical data, and decides what to keep. Existing benchmarks score formulas, code, or individual trading decisions, leaving open whether large language models (LLMs) can complete this loop and produce strategies that work beyond the research period. We introduce Quant-Arena, a benchmark and public leaderboard for end-to-end quantitative research on real A-share market data. A model constructs features and stock-scoring code, receives feedback only on 2022--2023, and freezes one strategy for assessment on the sealed year 2024. A cash-account evaluator applies trading constraints and costs, including market impact estimated from a simulated order book. Scores are dominated by realized excess return and complemented by signal quality and the diversity of features used by the strategy. We evaluate nine frontier models, among which GPT-6 Astra scores highest; yet no model beats the equal-weight market in 2024. Quant-Arena is open to submissions from any model or agent harness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://lab.einsia.ai/quant-arena</p></details> |
| **[Multimodal Embodied Agents: A Unified Capability-centric Perspective from Computer-Use to Robot-Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30108v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal agents (MMAs) sustain interaction through reasoning, memory, tools, and feedback, most visibly as computer-use agents, while robotic systems (RSs) couple sensing and actuation under physical dynamics. We define multimodal embodied agents (MMEAs) as goal-directed systems that couple multimodal task reasoning with physical action and revise decisions from the resulting feedback. This raises a central question: what changes when a multimodal agent moves from computer-use to robot-use? We introduce PAPAV, a unified capability-centric framework of five recurring functions: Perceive the current state, Anticipate action effects, Plan a feasible course, Act through an interface or body, and Verify the outcome. Defined by function rather than architecture, these capabilities let PAPAV identify what all three share and then compare MMEAs with MMAs and with RSs. Five physical constraints, from partial observability to unverifiable outcomes, leave fewer choices fixed in advance and less room to reverse errors. Across 64 benchmarks, explicit evaluation centers on Act; only 5 assess Anticipate and 3 assess Verify, leaving both largely hidden behind task success. These constraints also frame the open challenges we identify. PAPAV therefore provides a common basis for designing reliable physical agents and measuring progress across all five capabilities rather than task success alone.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://showlab.github.io/Awesome-Multimodal-Embodied-Agent/</p></details> |
| **[Did the Problem Actually Get Harder? Testing LLMs on Paired P and NP-Complete Problems Hardened by Adversarial Multi-Agent Rewriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30103v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many NP-complete (NPC) problems have a polynomial-time (P) twin that differs in a single structural detail, such as 3- versus 2-colouring a graph. A model that reasons rather than recalls should notice which side of this boundary a problem falls on, yet existing benchmarks either fix the wording of each task or ignore its complexity class, so they cannot separate the two. We introduce P2NPCBench, a benchmark of 12 problem families in which every NPC problem is paired with a P twin posed over the same surface. To remove the textbook cues that enable recall, we build it with AgentGAN, an adversarial multi-agent framework in which a generator rewrites each statement until a panel of frozen code models fails, while an executable validity gate keeps every rewrite answer- and class-preserving. The result is 2,114 hardened statements with hand-verified reference human solvers. Evaluating 12 proprietary and open-source models, we find that every model scores lower on NPC statements than on their P twins (35.4% vs. 63.8% pass@1), and that 95.1% of model-family pairs fail more often on the NPC side. A tenfold time limit raises NPC accuracy only from 28.5% to 31.9%, so failures stem from wrong or crashing programs rather than slow ones. Hardening also narrows the class gap from 24.8 to 14.6 points, showing that part of the apparent gap reflects familiarity with textbook phrasing rather than complexity class.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://github.com/Labib1610/complexity-benchmark</p></details> |
| **[The Best Programming Language for Tokenmaxxing: An Investigation of Coding Agent Behavior Across Programming Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22807v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although coding agents are now very effective in a variety of programming languages, this paper first shows that the cost (in tokens) can very significantly by programming language. We evaluate five recent models on programming problems in Python, Java, Rust, and OCaml. We carefully control for problem difficulty, and show that there can be stark variation in token consumption that is consistent across models. To understand why, we analyze both the structure and content of agent trajectories. First, we re-execute every intermediate solution and abstract each trajectory as a sequence of test-outcome vectors, then label the work between successive solutions. This reveals agents repeatedly producing noncompiling solutions in unfamiliar languages and revising solutions that already pass. Second, we analyze trajectory text, finding that agents plan solutions in code comments, distrust the provided tests in favor of inputs they invent, and sidestep unfamiliar target languages by prototyping in Python. Our results show that by-language token efficiency is a metric that should be considered when benchmarking and developing multilingual agents, and, for the tokenmaxxer, a guide to the most expensive language to work in.</p></details> |  |
| **[Parallel AI Agent Collaboration as Graph-Structured Distributed Block Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30074v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Parallel AI agents can produce useful local edits yet degrade a shared solution when their edits are combined. We model this coordination problem as discrete block optimization with an exactly evaluable objective and a known dependency graph. Independent sets certify compatible edits whose verified gains, measured against the same current solution, add exactly. A coordinator constructs a safe compatible candidate and can also evaluate interacting edits to capture favorable interactions. We prove that an optimal fractional coloring yields a merger that preserves a $1/χ_f(G)$ fraction of the total verified local improvement, where $χ_f(G)$ is the graph's fractional chromatic number. This guarantee is tight among mergers that select a subset of the same proposals. Under conditional local-improvement guarantees and a block error-bound condition, repeated collaboration converges geometrically in the objective gap. The rate separates task structure, agent allocation, and computation cost, yielding a linear program for agent assignment and an explicit time-cost trade-off. Code-repair experiments show that verified merging resolves conflicts between individually useful edits that cause naive merging to fail. On larger conflict-free tasks, parallel agents reduce modeled completion time at higher input-token-equivalent cost. On three public road networks, fractional integration retains 922 units of verified graph-cut improvement across 90 paired runs. For route-search agents sharing exclusive access to vehicle routes, fractional scheduling reduces measured wall time by a median of 15% across twelve paired runs, with route-cost changes varying across runs.</p></details> | 24 pages, 8 figures |
| **[Charts as Epistemic Actions for Experience-Driven Agentic Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30072v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data agents built on ReAct-style loops now execute complex analytical workflows over structured data, yet their intermediate reasoning still runs almost entirely through text. Human analysts instead move between numbers, tables, and charts, reading visual evidence to expose trends and anomalies that text summaries drop. We ask whether charts can serve as internal epistemic actions inside a data agent's reasoning loop, and how to teach an agent to use them. We first build \probe{}, a suite of 200 controlled tasks under four matched interface conditions, and find a persistent gap between what agents can read and what they choose to use. Agents score as well from self-generated charts as from numeric output, yet plotting access alone does not translate into consistent use. Task slices show that charts lead on temporal, pattern, and anomaly reasoning, while text leads on precise reading and conditional verification. We then propose \method{}, which distils two families of experience from conditioned trajectory rollouts. One family records how to select and verify analytical operations, and the other records when to invoke text, charts, or both. A trajectory judge scores outcomes and labels intermediate steps, and a validation gate admits only experience that improves held-out performance. On three held-out benchmarks with four LLM backbones, \method{} raises analytical accuracy and visual evidence quality over matched baselines, and backbones that never drew charts begin using them, all without touching model parameters.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 5 figures, 9 tables. Preprint</p></details> |
| **[Does Centralized Learning Help Embodied Agents Coordinate, and When Can a Tabular Study Tell?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17454v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tabular studies are a common way to compare independent and centralized multi-agent learners, because they avoid function approximation. A natural expectation is that centralized learning, which learns joint actions, helps agents coordinate, especially when their bodies make solo success hard. Earlier work tests this only in parts: it measures coordination for one learning method, compares methods with the agents' bodies fixed, or varies speed but reports only how well the team did. We test it in a predator-prey environment, where teammates cooperatively hunt together while two teams compete. We compare independent Q-learning (IQL) with central Q-learning (CQL) in all four predator and prey pairings, under limits on speed and stamina, on a 5x5 and an 8x8 grid trained for 200,000 episodes each, and check that the agents learn on both. We measure coordination directly, with predictions for the main measurement fixed in advance. In this task, where one predator can capture prey alone, we find three things. First, CQL costs its agents in both roles on both grids, and its predators coordinate no more than IQL's. Second, its one benefit comes from acting on the best joint action: this cut capture time by 42% on the small grid with faster prey and unlimited stamina, but helped nowhere on the larger grid, where the best joint action had mostly never been tried. Third, on the larger grid a tie-breaking default decided which method won, even though the agents had passed a learning check. A tabular comparison can therefore be trusted only if it also checks how much of the state space the agents covered and implements the methods alike. We propose simple checks for both.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 2 figures. Under peer review at AAMAS 2027</p></details> |
| **[SWE-DIVE: Multi-Turn Coding Agent Evaluation with Dynamic User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30047v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coding agents are typically evaluated using one-shot, well-formulated prompts. Benchmarks such as SWE-bench use existing GitHub issues as input for a coding agent and evaluate whether the agent successfully solves the task. However, in real-world use cases, prompts are often not as well-formulated or structured as existing GitHub issues. Additionally, a coding agent session is not limited to a single prompt; it typically involves a multi-turn interaction in which the user iteratively adds new tasks and requirements that the agent must fulfill. To benchmark these abilities, we developed SWE-DIVE, a dynamic, interactive validation and evaluation framework for benchmarking coding agents in a multi-turn environment. To achieve this, we introduce a user simulator that can simulate different behavioral patterns and communicate with the coding agent. We also introduce a conversation validator that evaluates the user simulator's outputs to determine whether they fulfill the initial requirements and guide the agent toward successfully solving the task. The entire framework is embedded in a Harbor environment, which orchestrates the coding agent, user simulator, and conversation validator, and executes the test environment to determine whether the agent successfully solves the task. Our results and source code are available in our GitHub repository (https://github.com/lorenzbrehme/swe-dive_usersim).</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 workshop "Grounded User Simulation for Model Evaluation and Training: Diversity, Fidelity, and Validity'' (non-archival)</p></details> |
| **[TARSphere: A Robot-Oriented Full-Stack Framework for Long-Horizon Embodied Agents in Evolving Physical and Social Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30043v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robots must act under physical and social constraints while adapting to changes in their environment and human needs. Existing embodied-agent systems often focus on specified goals or abstract execution into symbolic actions, limiting evaluation in an evolving world. We introduce TARSphere, a robot-oriented full-stack framework for physically and socially grounded long-horizon embodied agents. TARSphere-Infra runs robot policies in a physics simulator with embodied NPCs and constrained dialogue. An event-driven finite-state machine (FSM) uses completed actions and delivered information to update task states and evaluate outcomes. TARSphere-Harness, its embodied brain harness, lets a frozen multimodal large language model (MLLM) act through multi-level executable skills, using observations, hierarchical memory, and execution feedback to revise its decisions. On this foundation, agent-in-the-loop curation aligns task semantics, causal dependencies, and scene geometry to construct TARSphere-Bench: 360 episodes across 30 indoor scenes. Five paired conditions assess perceptual robustness, disruption adaptation, task update, human coordination, and intent understanding for navigate-and-report and fetch-and-deliver tasks. Our evaluation of 8 MLLMs shows that general-purpose models can outperform embodied models, while task updates and human interaction remain difficult. Frequent collisions and falls further motivate evaluating execution safety alongside task success, even with a shared action interface and decision budget.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 8 figures, 7 tables. Project page: https://xuyicheng-zju.github.io/TARSphere/ Code: https://github.com/xuyicheng-zju/TARSphere</p></details> |
| **[HGM-Dual: Lineage-Aware Guided Agent Harness Optimization for Long-Horizon Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30042v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Harness optimization, which improves an agent's prompts, tools, and workflows, is an increasingly effective way to enhance agents built on frozen or closed-source LLMs. Leading approaches such as the Darwin--Godel Machine and the Huxley--Godel Machine (HGM) rely on recursive self-improvement and were developed mainly for coding, where the competence needed to improve the agent coincides with the competence being improved. Planning tasks, in which an agent must produce a sequence of tool calls that jointly satisfies interacting constraints, break this alignment. There, open-ended self-modification repeatedly explores ineffective changes and consumes substantial evaluation budgets. We introduce HGM-Dual, a diagnostic-guided framework for budget-efficient harness evolution for planning agents. First, HGM-Dual represents agent evolution as lineage-aware context. This context records what each ancestor changed, which behaviors improved or regressed, and which modifications were ineffective, giving the editor explicit edit-outcome attribution instead of an unstructured archive of past agents. Second, HGM-Dual introduces error-conditioned dual-path child generation. For each selected parent, it generates a general child that preserves open-ended exploration and specialized children that target the harness components most likely responsible for the parent's highest-ranked constraint violations. HGM-Dual outperforms the strongest baseline by up to 14.45% relative improvement in case accuracy while matching HGM variants' final performance with 2x-4x fewer evaluation budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[Robust Soft Skill Adaptation via Behavioral Localization in Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30041v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text skills can extend the capabilities of tool-using agents, but long skill descriptions increase computational costs. Soft prompts provide a compact alternative, yet learning from entire successful trajectories may also introduce unnecessary behavioral changes. We find that the useful effect of a text skill is highly concentrated in a small subset of trajectory positions. Based on this observation, we propose Behavioral Localization, which compares the frozen agent with and without the text skill to identify positions where the skill both increases the likelihood of successful tokens and meaningfully changes the next token distribution. We use these localized positions to supervise a short soft prompt. Experiments on SpreadsheetBench with a frozen Qwen3.6-35B-A3B agent show that the top 10\% of trajectory positions capture 96.12\% of the total positive skill gain. In the shared prompt setting, selective supervision improves success from 30.36\% with full trajectory to 36.79\%. Under task-specific conditions, using the top 10\% localized positions achieves 57.38\%, outperforming both the full text skill and the on-policy OPCD baseline. These results show that a small set of behaviorally relevant trajectory positions can provide effective supervision of soft skills.</p></details> |  |
| **[What the Author Wrote, What the Agent Reads: Silent Cue Loss in Enterprise Document Ingestion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30026v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise agents act on documents. Before an agent can follow a policy, answer a compliance question or file a ticket, some ingestion layer must turn a Word file or PDF into tokens - and that layer is where an agent deployed in the wild silently loses information. We show that one layer is lost by every mainstream converter - the meaning an author encodes in how text looks - and that the loss makes agents confidently wrong rather than visibly incomplete. We build a task from 76 real policy documents: recover the requirements the author singled out, scored against the file's own OOXML properties by a deterministic matcher with no LLM judge. Formatting-blind ingestion collapses from micro F1 0.690 to 0.150 (docling) and 0.075 (plain text), a gap that replicates across six answering models from two vendors - five Claude models spanning the Opus, Sonnet and Haiku tiers, and OpenAI's GPT-5.6. An agent that cannot see the cue guesses, and docling's false-positive rate on requirements the author did not flag is four times ours. A span-level leave-one-out localises the loss to two cues - highlight (+0.302) and colour (+0.262), both Holm-adjusted p < 1e-4 - while bold, italic, monospace and strikethrough each sit within +/-0.003 of zero. The same asymmetry appears in PDF. Colour is also the cue no mainstream converter preserves and no parsing benchmark scores, and it is common: 32.8% of 6,460 documents carry a deliberate colour or highlight, 10.3% on an obligation. A pre-registered test shows that typing a cue for the agent adds +0.085 beyond merely preserving it (Holm-adjusted p = 0.049). We validate the labels against rendered page images through an independent modality (kappa = 0.72) and bound what wording alone can achieve (AUC 0.664). For agent builders the practical finding is that ingestion, not reasoning, is where this reliability is won or lost, and that the fix is cheap and measurable.</p></details> | 28 pages, 5 figures |
| **[GuideEvolve: Self-Evolving Agent Framework with Expert Guidance and 3Ts Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30017v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study introduces GuideEvolve, a self-evolving agent framework incorporating expert guidance and three autonomous regulatory mechanisms, collectively termed the 3Ts: Think Fast and Slow, Throwback, and Termination by choice. The framework addresses critical limitations in extant self-evolving agent paradigms through dual innovations: (1) a task-specific expert-evaluation-driven evolutionary pipeline, and (2) the integration of 3Ts mechanisms to facilitate cognitive optimization, performance recovery via temporal rollback, and adaptive termination protocols. Empirical validation across the MGSM and MMLU benchmarks demonstrates that GuideEvolve attains performance metrics comparable to current state-of-the-art models (89.8% accuracy versus Gödel Agent's 90.6% on MGSM; 89.1% versus 87.9% on MMLU), while exhibiting notably reduced variance (25-59%) and enhanced robustness. Furthermore, the framework achieves a 100% evolution success rate, superior to the baseline's 82%, diminishes required optimization iterations by approximately 50% (from 30 iterations in baseline to 15.1 and 13.4 across tasks), and provides improved stability for production deployment.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 10 images/figures</p></details> |
| **[From Text to Voice: A Reproducible and Verifiable Framework for Evaluating Tool Calling LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15104v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Voice agents increasingly require reliable tool use from speech, whereas prominent tool-calling benchmarks remain text-based. We study whether verified text benchmarks can be converted into controlled audio-based tool calling evaluations without re-annotating the tool schema and gold labels. Our dataset-agnostic framework uses text-to-speech, speaker variation, and environmental noise to create paired text-audio instances while preserving the original dataset annotations. Based on extensive evaluation of 7 omni-modal models on audio-converted versions of Confetti and When2Call, our framework demonstrates that the performance is strongly model- and task-dependent: Gemini-3.1-Flash-Live obtains the highest Confetti score (70.4), whereas GPT-Realtime-1.5 performs best on When2Call (71.9). On Confetti, the text-to-voice gap ranges from 1.8 points for Qwen3-Omni to 4.8 points for GPT-Realtime-1.5. A targeted analysis of failure cases demonstrates that degradations most often reflect misunderstandings of argument values in the speech. Considering real-world deployment scenarios, we further report text-only results, an ambiguity-based reformulation stress test, and a reference-free LLM-as-judge protocol validated against human preferences. Notably, we find that open-source Qwen3 judges with at least 8B parameters exceed 80% agreement with proprietary judges, supporting privacy-preserving evaluation. Overall, our framework provides a verifiable and reproducible first-stage diagnostic that complements purpose-built audio corpora.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR). The final version will appear here: https://openreview.net/forum?id=69MeTLVKze</p></details> |
| **[Agent Behavior Maps: Locating Agents by What They Would Do, Without Running Them](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30014v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents with similar success rates can follow different action sequences. An Agent Behavior Map represents LLM-harness combinations using centered action log-likelihoods on shared reference trajectories, without requiring new environment rollouts from the target agent. We derive error bounds relating squared coordinate distances to trajectory KL under explicit assumptions, and across eight cross-scored models the distances track the directly measured trajectory KL in rank order. Across 100 LLMs and four environments, ALFWorld-based coordinates support performance prediction and identify behavioral similarities. In ALFWorld, these similarities persist after controlling for success rates and model size, including across model families. As an extension, harness-component displacements approximately compose, with accuracy varying across components and environments.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 5 figures, 12 tables. Under review</p></details> |
| **[Remembering Through Change: Provenance-Preserving Hypergraph Memory for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29995v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term memory for multimodal agents remains challenging, as it must preserve fine-grained visual evidence while tracking entity evolution across sessions. Existing abstraction-based memories compress images into text, often discarding visually discriminative details, whereas pixel-preserving memories retain raw observations but struggle to reliably associate diverse observations with persistent entity identities and track their evolving states. To address these limitations, we propose MHG-Mem, a multimodal hypergraph memory that jointly organizes entity identity, state evolution, and provenance to visual evidence. Specifically, MHG-Mem represents distinct entity mentions and their observed facts as individual nodes, grouping identity-consistent mentions and facts through occurrence hyperedges. It then synthesizes aspect-specific evolution states from these grouped observations and links them to supporting entities, facts, and source evidence through evolutionary and evidential hyperedges. To navigate this structured memory, we introduce hypergraph-based agentic search, which adaptively selects an entry layer, traverses typed hyperedges across memory levels, and inspects source observations when fine-grained verification is required. These designs support both detailed visual verification and reasoning over cross-session state changes. Extensive experiments across three benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of MHG-Mem, improving performance by 8.20-9.98% over the strongest baseline.</p></details> | 31 pages,10 figures |
| **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under open licenses at https://github.com/idiap/dialfork, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.</p></details> |  |
| **[CORD: Conflict-Aware Optimization for Joint RL and Distillation in Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29965v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Joint reinforcement learning and self-distillation combines task rewards with dense teacher guidance for agent training, but their gradients can partly cancel. A mixing coefficient controls relative scale without selectively removing opposing components. We present CORD, a general framework for conflict-aware optimization of two objectives. CORD separates the choice of protected objective from the condition that triggers projection, and combines the gradients once per optimizer mini-batch. A nearest-point formulation yields a closed-form update and characterizes its first-order effect on the protected objective before optimizer transformations. Gradient diagnostics connect conflict in agent training to lost local optimization progress and motivate selective correction. As a gradient-combination mechanism, CORD can be added to different joint training recipes while retaining their objectives. Across three such recipes, it improves ALFWorld success by 1.5-15.9 percentage points. On WebShop, it improves two of the three pairs, with SDAR rising from 60.7% to 64.6% success. Our analyses explain why protection direction and projection gate must be chosen together, and why the distillation objective and its mixing weight remain consequential after projection.</p></details> | 38 pages, 12 figures |
| **[ToM2Vec: Continuous Theory-of-Mind Tokens for Multi-Agent Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29934v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Forming an accurate theory-of-mind (ToM) is key to successful multi-agent collaboration and requires reasoning under uncertainty to capture multiple possible partner states. This is hard to do in the text representation space native to large language model (LLM) agents, which more easily describe a single partner state. To address this challenge, we introduce ToM2Vec, which learns to form abstract theories-of-mind in a rich latent space via a fixed budget of continuous reasoning tokens. During training, supervision for these tokens comes from the partner's actual thoughts, which the ToM tokens are trained to abstract and match. At test-time, ToM tokens are generated by each agent and act as conditioners for that agent's thoughts and actions. Across several environments covering collaborative, competitive, and mixed-motive settings (Overcooked-AI, multiparty negotiation, Decrypto, Hanabi, and Collab Escape/Capture), we evaluate ToM2Vec's ability to support multi-agent interactions and collaboration, finding consistent improvements over the base model and a variety of competitive trained and prompted baselines. We show that latent reasoning is critical, with performance dropping when reasoning is expressed in text. Moreover, we show that ToM2Vec training generalizes to partner model families not seen at train-time, including a proprietary model (GPT-5.4). | **[Privacy Preference Controls for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29929v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In response to vast and widespread online tracking, regulators and technologists alike have worked to develop laws and tools that allow people to express their privacy preferences. In this study, we ask: What should privacy preference controls look like in the agentic web and agentic systems more generally? To answer this question, we develop a taxonomy of enforceable privacy preferences for agentic systems. Our taxonomy is informed both by laws and empirical studies of existing privacy tools as well as an audit of 30 leading AI assistants and agent platforms. To explore how our taxonomy can be implemented, we design and develop eight open-source prototypes that mirror real-world agentic applications and demonstrate how major aspects of our taxonomy can be structurally integrated in agentic protocols, such as the Model Control Protocol and Agent2Agent protocol. Ultimately, our study seeks to provide the groundwork for ensuring end-user privacy and autonomy is not lost in the newly arriving agentic world.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, 4 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |

| **[SUDP: Secret-Use Delegation Protocol for Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24920v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems now act with user secrets for APIs, messaging platforms, and cloud services. Existing defenses cover adjacent pieces such as secret storage, scoped delegation, sender-constrained tokens, and runtime monitoring, but none states the combined requirement: an untrusted autonomous requester should be able to cause a user-authorized secret-backed operation without gaining reusable authority over it. We formalize this as the Agent Secret Use (ASU) problem and identify seven security properties any solution must satisfy, spanning authorization integrity and secret confidentiality. We propose the Secret-Use Delegation Protocol (SUDP), in which a requester proposes a canonical operation, the user authorizes it with a fresh authenticator-backed grant, and a custodian redeems the grant to perform the bounded use; reusable authority never crosses the requester boundary. We specialize SUDP for LLM-driven agents, where it applies whenever a tool call would exercise user-enrolled authority-bearing material. Under standard cryptographic assumptions, SUDP satisfies six of the seven properties; the seventh, runtime-memory confidentiality, is closed by composition with a hardware-rooted runtime. A reference implementation, available at https://github.com/xhyumiracle/sudp, adds sub-millisecond protocol overhead per mediated call.</p></details> |  |

| **[2-Step Agent: How a Bayesian Decision Maker Learns from AI-Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21889v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prognostic predictions from ML models support human decision making in several fields, including high-stakes ones such as healthcare and the judiciary. Together, these components reframe agent memory from passive lookup to continuous learning, offering a principled route toward self-improving autonomous agents.</p></details> |  |

| **[Workflow-Aware Communication and Computation Scheduling for Mobile Edge AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29337v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly deployed in mobile edge environments to autonomously handle diverse tasks through multi-step workflows. To address the uncertainty of future workflow evolution, we first transform the weighted completion time objective into an equivalent per-slot holding cost and characterize the optimal policy through a Bellman equation. We then construct a tractable look-ahead cost-to-go surrogate based on the revealed workflow and the estimated remaining latencies of active jobs. The proposed scheduling algorithm leverages the surrogate to determine computation placement and uses the resulting operation criticality to allocate wireless and edge computing resources online. | **[Agentic Video Understanding: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31713v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) become capable of processing increasingly diverse modalities and longer temporal contexts, an emerging line of work is moving beyond fixed video-language inference toward agentic systems that actively decide what information to inspect, retain, verify, and act upon. ## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Distillation, Self-Training, and the Fragility of Learned Chemical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24199v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automating synthesis planning for small-molecule drug design requires chemical reasoners that are accurate as well as open to inspection and adaptation, yet frontier language models remain unreliable at predicting reaction outcomes and difficult to deploy, while small open-weight models lack the necessary domain reasoning. Our models, Resonance, distill an open-weight teacher's reasoning traces and then learn from their own verified outputs, turning a small base model with near-zero accuracy into a capable reaction predictor, while a stronger student surpasses its teacher through distillation alone. Accuracy, however, does not bring robust reasoning: the small student inherits its teacher's single-pass style, self-training mainly sharpens answers it could already reach, and it readily abandons correct answers under pushback, suggesting that chemical reasoning models should be evaluated on the robustness of their reasoning as well as on the accuracy of their answers.</p></details> | 10 pages, 7 figures |
| **[On the Selectivity of Generative Models in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24124v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing small-molecule drugs that selectively bind to a target protein while avoiding unintended interactions with other proteins is essential for reducing side effects that contribute substantially to clinical attrition. Although structure-based drug design (SBDD) has advanced rapidly with deep learning, most generative models still optimize binding to a single target and disregard selectivity due to limited training data and unknown off-target interactions. Motivated by this gap, we introduce SELECTBENCH, a comprehensive benchmark assessing the selectivity of generative SBDD models. SELECTBENCH provides three curated evaluation tasks reflecting different real-world drug discovery scenarios: literature-based case studies, safety screening panels used in pharmaceutical development, and large-scale structure-based evaluation sets. We further introduce a suite of metrics to quantify selectivity and conduct a systematic evaluation of nine representative SBDD models, including methods explicitly trained for selectivity and widely-used inference-time guidance techniques. Our results show that even selectivity-trained models achieve only modest off-target discrimination and inference-time guidance provides limited gains, underscoring the need for method improvement. SELECTBENCH provides both a characterization of where current generative SBDD stands on selectivity and an open, extensible platform to accelerate progress. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/lovetaro/selectbench.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a Presentation at the Neural Information Processing Systems 2026</p></details> |

Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. We compose the reward from a conditional value-at-risk (CVaR), a worst-case score rule, and an ambiguity radius expressing distrust in the stated weights. An exact-KL oracle, a copy trained on the true target, shows if a shortfall is the optimiser's or the architecture's. When good candidates are rare, exploration decides: injecting unseen states finds 0.987-1.000 of good regions, while reweighting visited finds 0.35-0.76.</p></details> | <details><summary>Paper...</summary><p>Paper submitted to the Northern Lights Deep Learning Conference (NLDL) 2027</p></details> |
| **[Language-Informed Flow Matching for Trend-Guided Structure-Based 3D Molecular Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31009v1)** | 2026-08-31 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design (SBDD) requires ligands that satisfy both 3D target affinity and 1D chemical validity. Supplementary material is included as an ancillary file. Accepted for publication in IEEE Transactions on Computational Biology and Bioinformatics</p></details> |

Preprint</p></details> |

| **[Latent Style-based Quantum Wasserstein GAN for Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22399v1)** | 2026-03-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The development of new drugs is a tedious, time-consuming, and expensive process, for which the average costs are estimated to be up to around $2.5 billion. Reinforcement Learning (RL) enhances this process by enabling multi-objective optimization and exploration of novel chemical space - capabilities that traditional supervised learning methods lack. In this work, we introduce \textbf{ReACT-Drug}, a fully integrated, target-agnostic molecular design framework based on Reinforcement Learning. Unlike models requiring target-specific fine-tuning, ReACT-Drug utilizes a generalist approach by leveraging ESM-2 protein embeddings to identify similar proteins for a given target from a knowledge base such as Protein Data Base (PDB). Thereafter, the known drug ligands corresponding to such proteins are decomposed to initialize a fragment-based search space, biasing the agent towards biologically relevant subspaces. For each such fragment, the pipeline employs a Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) agent guiding a ChemBERTa-encoded molecule through a dynamic action space of chemically valid, reaction-template-based transformations. This results in the generation of \textit{de novo} drug candidates with competitive binding affinities and high synthetic accessibility, while ensuring 100\% chemical validity and novelty as per MOSES benchmarking. This architecture highlights the potential of integrating structural biology, deep representation learning, and chemical synthesis rules to automate and accelerate rational drug design. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/YadunandanRaman/ReACT-Drug/.</p></details> |  |

## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[LOADBench: A Large Open Analog Dataset and Benchmark for Machine Learning in IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21591v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. We address both gaps with a large opensource dataset and a suite of standardized benchmarks. The dataset contains more than 100 operational amplifier (opamp) topologies (1-, 2-, and 3-stage) in the SkyWater130 PDK, each shipped with structural and functional annotations, topology-specific sizing constraints (symmetry and sizing rules), an Ngspice testbench, and a multi-objective NSGA-II sizing run producing around 103 Pareto-optimal and 106 intermediate sizing-performance pairs per topology. On top of this data we define six benchmark tasks with precise protocols: inverse sizing, topology selection, sub-circuit classification, structural circuit generation, Pareto-membership prediction, and multi-objective optimization. Thus, the data presented here constitutes the most comprehensive dataset for ML development in over four decades of analog IC design automation. The dataset, ground-truth annotations, and reference implementations of the metric-defining routines are released under an open license, enabling reproducible and directly comparable evaluation across heterogeneous analog design automation methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>Datas...</summary><p>Dataset available on zenodo: https://zenodo.org/records/21759759 9 pages, 6 figures, Conference: MLCAD '26: 8th ACM/IEEE International Symposium on Machine Learning for CAD, September 7 to 9, 2026, Jeju, South Korea</p></details> |
| **[BIRD-RL: Scaling Agentic Reinforcement Learning over Stateful Data-Centric Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29426v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While agentic reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for training small language models (SLMs), existing frameworks are predominantly tool-centric and struggle with data-centric environments. Specifically, applying tool-centric frameworks to stateful tasks like SQL debugging introduces severe spatial inefficiencies since proper environment isolation typically requires a separate database container per rollout. Naively reusing databases without tracking evolving states across multi-turn interactions causes trajectory contamination and cross-trajectory interference, which severely destabilizes policy updates. To overcome these bottlenecks, we introduce BIRD-RL, a novel four-stage agentic RL curriculum that alternately enhances foundational agent reasoning and trajectory-adaptive on-policy exploration to circumvent the instability inherently associated with direct end-to-end learning. Crucially, to support this curriculum, BIRD-RL is driven by a Trajectory-scoped Persistent Execution Infrastructure, which enables safe database replica reuse while preserving trajectory isolation. This design improves spatial RL efficiency by 12.8x, successfully enabling scalable agentic RL for complex data-centric tasks without needs of Kubernettes. Empirical evaluations on the BIRD-CRITIC-SQLite benchmark demonstrate that our 7B and 14B models achieve Success Rates of 44.40% and 48.00%, respectively, showing competitive performance against strong proprietary model-based agents such as Claude-Opus-4.6 Agent (48.20%). Furthermore, BIRD-RL successfully trains unified SLMs capable of both advanced SQL debugging and generation, highlighting its broader potential for multi-objective optimization across heterogeneous, real-world data-centric tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 Main Track</p></details> |

This suggests that LLM depth pruning should be formulated as a multi-objective optimization problem rather than a single-score ranking problem. We propose SliMOO, an interpretable multi-objective evolutionary search framework for LLM depth pruning. SliMOO first uses single-layer removal probing to construct a task-layer importance map, which provides an interpretable view of task-shared and task-specific layers and serves as a search prior. It then performs a Pareto-aware evolutionary search for pruning masks, where candidates are generated under the guidance of the task-layer prior, scored by their deviation from the dense model on each task, and retained through NSGA-II environmental selection. Across the Llama-3.1 and Qwen3 model families, SliMOO consistently achieves stronger multi-task trade-offs than rule-based, greedy, and scalarized baselines. For example, on Llama-3.1-70B at 25% sparsity, it improves the math-domain average from 24.33% to 34.40% and the code-domain average from 35.03% to 39.14%. Code will be made available at https://github.com/AMD-AGI/SliMOO.</p></details> |  |
| **[Jacobian Descent for Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.16232v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many optimization problems require balancing multiple conflicting objectives. While several methods to combine gradients already exist in the literature, they generally struggle when the objectives conflict. In contrast, we propose projecting gradients to fully resolve conflict. We prove convergence to the Pareto front in the smooth convex case with this approach, a stronger guarantee than the usual convergence to a weakly Pareto stationary point. Our method also enables instance-wise risk minimization (IWRM), a novel learning paradigm in which the loss of each training example is considered a separate objective. Early experimentation on image classification shows promising results for IWRM. Lastly, we provide an efficient implementation of JD using the Gramian of the Jacobian matrix to drastically reduce memory requirements.</p></details> | <details><summary>53 pa...</summary><p>53 pages, 14 figures, Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster)</p></details> |

By employing a dual-parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) architecture, we design a channel-aware tiered transmission mechanism that adaptively truncates payloads to maximize node survival rates under fading conditions. Although there is a concept named \emph{empirical attainment surface} to enable the visualization with uncertainty over multiple runs, there is no major Python package for empirical attainment surface. We, therefore, develop a Python package for this purpose and describe the usage. The package is available at https://github.com/nabenabe0928/empirical-attainment-func. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. | **[Progressive Lower Bound Evaluation for Urban Road Renovation Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15554v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban infrastructure degrades over time, necessitating periodic renovation to maintain functionality and safety. | **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

A novel generalized Run-Length Limited (RLL) constraint is introduced to bridge the structural mismatch between the DNA encoding domain and the label error domain. \item \textbf{Asymptotic optimality.} The proposed scheme achieves redundancy $\log_4 n + (t-1)\log_4 \log_{8/3} n + \mathcal{O}(1)$, matching the dominant term of the lower bound up to a small $\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)$ overhead, rendering the construction asymptotically optimal for fixed $t$. \end{itemize}</p></details> |  |

Our results demonstrate that deconvolution accuracy is highly sensitive to latent component structure and show that explicitly modeling this heterogeneity yields substantial improvements over standard whole-genome deconvolution strategies. To complement our non-constructive redundancy upper bound, we design efficiently and encodable and decodable codes for any constant $t$. In particular, for $t=1$ and $C>1/2$ we construct efficient codes with redundancy that essentially matches our non-constructive upper bound; b) $k$ equal a constant; in this case we consider the extremal problem where the number of deletions is not bounded and a deletion is imposed after every run of length at least $k$, which we call the extremal contextual deletion channel. This combinatorial setting arises naturally by considering a probabilistic channel that introduces contextual deletions after each run of length at least $k$ with probability $p$ and taking the limit $p\to 1$. | **[A Rigid Category of DNA Secondary Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12740v1)** | 2026-05-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We construct a strict pivotal monoidal category $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{DNA}}$ whose objects are DNA sequences (words over $\{A,C,G,T\}$) and whose morphisms are isotopy classes of typed noncrossing planar matchings, composed of through-strands and Watson-Crick-typed arcs, in a rectangle with source and target boundaries. The dual of a sequence is its reverse complement, evaluation and coevaluation are canonical duplex pairings, and the snake identities hold by planar isotopy. A bending correspondence identifies each morphism $x \to y$ with a secondary structure on the combined word $x{}^{\vee} y$; in particular, the generalized elements $\varepsilon \to w$ are exactly the non-pseudoknotted secondary structures on $w$. Composition, viewed in this straightened picture, is computed by a zip-and-transfer operation on complementary interfaces, a combinatorial rearrangement of base-pair connectivity of which toehold-mediated strand displacement is a kinetically specific instance. Because $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{DNA}}$ is rigid monoidal, it shares the categorical backbone of pregroup grammars and the DisCoCat framework for compositional semantics: a strong monoidal functor from a grammatical category to $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{DNA}}$ maps grammatical reductions to Watson-Crick base pairing and sentence meanings to secondary structures. We describe this functor and discuss connections to algorithmic self-assembly, composable strand-displacement circuits, and constructive dynamical systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, multiple figures (TikZ); submitted to Applied Category Theory 2026</p></details> |

| **[LongZero: Enhancing Long-Context Reasoning in LLMs from Zero Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30132v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) is an effective way to strengthen the long-context reasoning of large language models (LLMs), but it needs training data that are long, diverse, challenging, and verifiable. Human annotation of such data is expensive, naturally occurring data are too scarce to sustain RL, and existing synthesis methods depend on external corpora or stronger teacher models, which become harder to obtain as the trained models get stronger. Recently, RL from zero data, in which a model learns from tasks it proposes itself, has achieved excellent performance, but existing methods target short-context tasks. Therefore, we propose $\textbf{LongZero}$, a method that lets an LLM improve its own long-context reasoning without any external data. Instead of writing questions and answers over existing documents, LongZero first has the model synthesize the question and answer with its supporting evidence in a compact structured form, and only then separately expands the evidence into a long multi-document context. LongZero combines these self-written multi-hop reasoning tasks with self-written multi-round retrieval tasks and trains the model with GRPO. We evaluate LongZero on two LLMs across five long-context reasoning benchmarks and compare it with RL on data synthesized from external corpora. Experimental results show that LongZero can generalize to real tasks, and outperforms both baselines, suggesting a viable and promising path to improving long-context reasoning of LLMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code and data are available at https://github.com/zhennan1/LongZero</p></details> |
| **[Environment Duels: A Microcosm of LLM Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30116v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Through a tangle of real-world interactions, from synthetic training pipelines to data shared on the open web, frontier LLMs absorb what their predecessors and competitors create. How well does each model play its role in this ecosystem, as a creator and as a solver? We introduce Environment Duels, a dynamic agentic benchmark that evaluates both capabilities on interactive environments. Each model writes environments, each with a privileged hint, aiming for tasks that capable solvers fail without the hint and pass with it. Across nine frontier LLMs, solving and authoring prove to be distinct skills: the strongest solvers are not the strongest authors, while smaller models learn more from how others played and write the hardest environments and the most useful hints. Beyond the scores, the pairwise interactions form a microcosm of how models affect one another. Head-to-head outcomes are transitive and so yield a stable ranking; independent authors converge on the same designs; and in a reinforcement-learning study with Qwen3.8-27B, the learner's hint lift on an author's environments tracks how much training on those environments improves it on held-out reasoning and agentic benchmarks, whether the environments are its own or another model's. Each of these patterns has a counterpart in how models shape one another outside the duel. By staging these interactions under controlled conditions, Environment Duels offers a window onto how LLMs teach, challenge and learn from one another. Code is available at https://github.com/LLM-Duels/EnvDuels.</p></details> |  |
| **[Quant-Arena: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Quantitative Trading Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30113v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Quantitative research is an iterative loop: a researcher proposes a market hypothesis, implements it as an executable signal, tests it on historical data, and decides what to keep. Existing benchmarks score formulas, code, or individual trading decisions, leaving open whether large language models (LLMs) can complete this loop and produce strategies that work beyond the research period. We introduce Quant-Arena, a benchmark and public leaderboard for end-to-end quantitative research on real A-share market data. A model constructs features and stock-scoring code, receives feedback only on 2022--2023, and freezes one strategy for assessment on the sealed year 2024. A cash-account evaluator applies trading constraints and costs, including market impact estimated from a simulated order book. Scores are dominated by realized excess return and complemented by signal quality and the diversity of features used by the strategy. We evaluate nine frontier models, among which GPT-6 Astra scores highest; yet no model beats the equal-weight market in 2024. Quant-Arena is open to submissions from any model or agent harness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://lab.einsia.ai/quant-arena</p></details> |
| **[Circuits of LLM Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30076v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language models make it easy to build classifiers by simply prompting a model with the sample to classify. However, their predictions are neither deterministic nor uniformly reliable across samples. How can we combine predictions from different models to improve reliability? To this end, we introduce circuits that organize LLM classifiers into an adaptive sequence of queries and decisions. We then characterize the optimal circuit, obtain the fundamental limits of achievable accuracy, and provide practical insights on realizing the optimal circuit. Across seven classification tasks spanning safety, health, political science, human preference, agent evaluation, decision-making and reasoning, our method improves the cost-accuracy frontier over routing and cascading baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Hitchhiker's Guide to Program Analysis, Part III: Mostly Harmless LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15122v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Filtering out a static-analysis warning asserts that no feasible execution can trigger the reported error, whereas confirming a bug requires only one that does. Recent work uses LLMs to filter out warnings. However, LLM-generated explanations alone cannot establish that the reported error is impossible, and mistakenly filtering out a warning conceals a real bug. Sound static analysis can provide this guarantee, but scalable analyses leave behind the very warnings we seek to filter, whereas sufficiently precise whole-program analysis is impractical. Fortunately, analyzing a small part of the program is often enough to show that a warning is a false alarm. We therefore assign LLMs a different role: rather than judging the validity of warnings, they identify relevant code and construct a harness that supplies the context needed for precise, targeted static analysis. We present Evident, which combines LLMs with the static analyzer Frama-C/Eva. Because a generated harness may exclude inputs or paths that trigger real bugs, Evident restricts how the harness initializes inputs and validates it using the value flow reported by the original detector. A warning is filtered out only when validation passes and Eva rules out the reported error in the harness; otherwise, the warning is kept. On 200 real Android kernel-driver warnings, including 40 confirmed bugs, Evident filters out 48.8-63.8% of the 160 false alarms across four LLMs without missing any confirmed bug, whereas baselines that let LLMs make the final decision miss confirmed bugs with every evaluated model. With GPT-5.5, although these baselines achieve 2.0-5.5 percentage points higher overall accuracy by filtering out more false alarms, they miss 27.5-45.0% of the confirmed bugs. Evident also rediscovers a confirmed vulnerability that had previously been overlooked by both LLM-based filtering and manual review.</p></details> |  |

Considering real-world deployment scenarios, we further report text-only results, an ambiguity-based reformulation stress test, and a reference-free LLM-as-judge protocol validated against human preferences. | **[An important Bottleneck in LLM Optimizers: Principle-Level Local Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24291v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Feedback-driven LLM optimizers are increasingly used for evolutionary search, Bayesian optimization, heuristic design, and scientific discovery. We assemble a diagnostic benchmark spanning molecular optimization, network sentinel-node selection, and GPU-kernel optimization, and introduce a decision-tree protocol combining principle narrowing, prolonged performance plateaus, and gaps to attainable reference solutions. Across 161 assessable runs on the three problem domains, 78.3\% receive support for collapse, including 55.3\% with strong support. Candidate-level variation can nevertheless conceal restricted search at the principle level. We then test \emph{diversity injection}, a common response to search stagnation, such as higher t, multiple LLMs, and web search, and show that it provides limited and inconsistent benefits: among 65 molecular runs, still 73.8\% and 53.8\% receive support and strong support respectively. We finally test reference-principle interventions and find that they improve some runs, but their benefits vary across models and tasks: access to a better principle does not guarantee its effective use. Our benchmark and diagnostic protocol provide a basis for evaluating future optimizers on principle-level collapse. Our findings highlight the need for online collapse detection and better mechanisms for generating and maintaining alternative principles during optimization.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages under review</p></details> |
| **[EPIC: Rethinking Trust Regions for Autoregressive LLM Policies via Exact-KL Prefix Importance-Ratio Clipping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29999v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) based on PPO-style clipping has become an effective approach to improving the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Experiments on off-policy reinforcement learning with a sparse mixture-of-experts language model show that EPIC consistently outperforms standard and sequence-level clipping baselines across mathematical-reasoning benchmarks, while remaining competitive with routing-replay-based alternatives. | **[HiddenLens: A Multi-Layer LLM Framework for Detecting and Resolving Hidden Assumptions in Software Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29996v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Natural-language software requirements often omit assumptions about performance, operating conditions, user behaviour, and domain constraints, affecting architecture, testing, and deployment. If undiscovered until implementation, resolving them is far more costly than addressing them early. To evaluate CORE in realistic settings, we introduce ProductBench, a large-scale benchmark with 15 product categories and 200 products per category, where each product is associated with its top-10 recommendations collected from Amazon's search interface. | **[Reasoning-Search-Augmented LLMs: An Operational Taxonomy, Benchmark Analysis and Controlled Evidence Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29189v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate impressive emergent reasoning capabilities, yet they remain constrained by fixed model parameters and finite context windows. We analyze these systems through four architectural axes: retrieval coupling, controller design, knowledge source, and grounding strictness. We separately treat the learning signal as a cross-cutting dimension because prompting, supervision, preference optimization, and reinforcement learning can instantiate the same architecture while producing materially different search, stopping, and grounding behavior. We further distinguish benchmark difficulty from RSA-loop demand and synthesize controlled within-study ablations and quality-cost deltas, avoiding invalid pooling across heterogeneous model backbones, retrievers, corpora, and evaluation protocols. A merge is admitted only when the child moves toward the donor, past the child the same operator produces at zero dose, and the lower confidence bound on that movement reaches half the requested dose. That is the admission test. A detectable shortfall rejects the operator, and the test is otherwise undetermined. We keep realised fitness φas the selector and reference performance θas the unused ruler. Every measurement uses gemini-3.1-flash-lite on one negotiation task, and no fixed-oracle arm was run. In the open loop, tactic append grafts short donor rules and is admitted. Free rewrite moves only a third of the requested dose and is rejected. Template append pastes raw donor replies into the child. Its movement clears the bound on the first probe but not on the re-probe, and it is left undetermined. A held-out re-probe on the five situations the merge never saw confirms that both appends move, and it does not rescue free rewrite. Neither append rises with the dose. Constrained rewrite is admitted in the open loop and was not run in the closed loop, which changes only the operator. The population, the scores and the task stay fixed. No free-rewrite run beats the best generation-zero member. Every tactic-append run and every template-append run does. Sampled loop children keep that split. Because manually performing this task is time- and labor-intensive, recent work has turned to LLMs as judges to automate the process, referred to as automated failure attribution (AFA). In practice, however, direct prompting often performs poorly, which motivates follow-up work that explores various ways to improve AFA performance, including alternative prompting strategies and inference-time interventions. In this paper, we examine a more fundamental question: whether this weak behavioral performance reflects a genuine capability limitation. Using linear probes applied to internal activations, we uncover a significant \emph{knows-but-won't-say gap:} models encode sufficient information to attribute a failure correctly but fail to explicitly output the responsible step. Building on this finding, we propose \emph{grafting}, a simple manual intervention that exploits knowledge already encoded in the model to improve AFA performance without requiring ground-truth labels at inference time. Extensive evaluations across multiple LLMs and benchmark settings demonstrate that grafting consistently outperforms direct-prompting approaches, underscoring the potential of leveraging latent model knowledge for more reliable failure attribution. Our code is available at https://github.com/wendyzheng22/mi4afa.</p></details> |  |
| **[Combining Long-Term Memory and LLMs Comes at a High Cost: From Prompt Injection to Persistent Compromise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28810v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The widespread adoption of generative artificial intelligence in recent years has brought a parallel rise in prompt injection and jailbreak attacks. However, persistent memory features in modern conversational interfaces enable cross-session attack propagation, establishing a threat vector known as memory injection. This work explores this emerging vulnerability in leading LLM-as-a-service platforms, highlighting its real-world severity and the need for countermeasures. While existing literature focuses on violations tailored to specific platform features, we present a general threat model. We demonstrate that all NIST attack goals are collectively achievable across ChatGPT Instant 5.3, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Gemini 3 Flash under this model. We also show that memory injections can exploit multiple attack vectors, including personal memories, projects, skills, organization instructions, and Model Context Protocol (MCP)-connected resources. Our findings reveal that all evaluated services can be manipulated to persistently spread disinformation, generate harmful content, or simulate offline unresponsiveness, even in fresh sessions. In the particular case of Claude, shared-memory features allow an attacker to compromise multiple users within an organization and also exfiltrate user prompts. To address these vulnerabilities, we derive defense mechanisms from our proposed threat model and foundational cybersecurity principles. Our analysis indicates that nearly half of these mitigations remain unimplemented across the target services. | **[How Much Does the Harness Matter, and for Whom? | **[Where Do Legal Factors Live in LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28799v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In case-based legal reasoning, legal professionals identify legally significant fact patterns in a new dispute and compare them with those of decided cases. Results show that all 26 factors are linearly decodable in all three models. Single-feature sparse readouts cover the complete factor catalog, with best-layer test AUCs ranging from 0.86 to 1.00. Five selected features approach full-vector readout performance. We report 78 model-specific layer-feature coordinates, one for each factor in each model. Together, these results show that information corresponding to expert-defined legal factors can be read from the internal activations of LLMs processing legal narratives. By locating these readouts, our study connects legal language and the symbolic abstractions of HYPO and CATO to learned internal representations.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 3 figures, 2 tables. Accepted at JURIX 2026, the 39th International Conference on Legal Knowledge and Information Systems</p></details> |
| **[Is Anyone Enforcing the Cartel? What Reinforcement Learning Teaches LLM Agents in Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28787v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluations of large language model (LLM) agents report that pricing agents collude at others' expense. Yet collusion is defined by enforcement: a cartel, in which competitors share monopoly profits, holds only if a member who cheats is punished. Small LLMs trained by RL learn what their training rivals reward, but only what they readily try. In self-play, they learn fixed rules ignoring their rivals; against a punishing rival, to be deterred; against a cheating rival, to match its cuts, never the costly price war that would deter it, which they rarely try. A war forced into training is learned, but mostly only where it was forced. Against their own copies, deterred and matching agents can earn the same cartel profits, but only matching ones can enforce them, and then thinly: as a by-product and only against large cuts. | **[When Dynamic Scaling Fails: A Scale-State Feedback Governs Recurrent-State Quantization in Linear-Attention and SSM LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15139v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Linear-attention and state-space LLMs carry long context in a fixed-size recurrent state, and quantizing that state is the natural way to cut its cost during long decode. INT4 that leaves synthetic recall perfectly intact at the start destroys it a thousand steps later. The mechanism is a feedback between the quantizer scale and the state it is derived from. Yoking that scale to a full-precision reference removes the resulting norm explosion entirely, yet recovers only 1.8 of the 5.4 nats lost, so numerical instability and functional degradation are separable failures. The remainder is resolution-dependent, and in our per-step setting every outlier-aware scheme we test loses to plain persistent scaling. Allocating bits across layers does help, but under article-disjoint splits a learned allocation collapses onto the trivial rule of protecting the first few layers. Run out to 32K, group-wise INT4 is worth no more than discarding the state. Contain the scale rather than locate outliers, and report the decode horizon at which a bit-width was certified.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 6 figures, 7 tables. NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Long-Context Foundation Models (non-archival). Extended version: adds a cross-architecture containment comparison on Mamba-2 and corrects two claims in the workshop version. v2 discusses three concurrent works and narrows two claims. | **[Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) often struggle with temporally grounded reasoning because their static parametric memory is misaligned with the dynamic evolution of real-world knowledge. Although RAG and Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) provide practical ways to enhance LLMs, they remain insufficient for temporal reasoning. Existing retrieval methods typically prioritize semantic relevance while overlooking temporal validity, event order, and evolutionary dependencies, resulting in evidence that is relevant in isolation but temporally fragmented.
